Enanta Pharmaceuticals 宣布 Zelicapavir 的 RESOLVE 2b/3 期研究中第一位患者接受给药,呼吸道合胞病毒 (RSV) 是 呼吸道合胞病毒 (RSV) 的潜在首例疾病患者

Enanta Pharmaceuticals Announces First Patient Dosed in the RESOLVE Phase 2b/3 Study of Zelicapavir, a Potential First-in-Disease Treatment for Respiratory Syncytial Virus (RSV)
新闻简报Enanta Pharmaceuticals 已于 2026 年 10 月 26 日启动了泽利卡帕韦(zelicapavir)的 RESOLVE 2b/3 期研究,泽利卡帕韦是治疗 呼吸道合胞病毒 (RSV) 感染的潜在 治疗,该研究于 2026 年 10 月 26 日对第一例患者进行给药。这项全球试验针对的是严重 RSV 结局高风险的成年人,包括 75 岁或以上、患有慢性阻塞性肺病 (COPD) 或充血性心力衰竭的成年人。该研究旨在评估泽利卡帕韦在减少症状持续时间和住院率方面的有效性和安全性。2b 期部分的主要数据预计将于 2027 年获得,3 期部分计划招募约 660 名患者。Zelicapavir 可抑制 RSV N 蛋白,已在 700 多名参与者中显示出良好的安全性,并可能为 RSV 提供首个疾病 抗病毒药物 治疗。该试验旨在确认 治疗 的效应大小并产生额外的临床证据,有可能对高危人群的 RSV 管理产生重大影响。
查看新闻五要素
时间
首例患者于 2026 年 10 月 26 日在 RESOLVE 2b/3 期研究中接受给药。Topline 2b 期数据预计将于 2027 年提供。
地点与对象
全球 2b/3 期临床试验针对 RSV 感染导致严重后果的高风险成人门诊患者,包括 75 岁或以上、患有慢性阻塞性肺病或充血性心力衰竭的患者。
事件
Enanta Pharmaceuticals 宣布了 zelicapavir(一种治疗 RSV 感染的潜在 治疗) RESOLVE 2b/3 期研究中第一位患者的用药。该研究旨在评估高危成人的症状持续时间和住院率。
规模、影响与风险
该研究的 2b 期部分将至少包括 200 名患者,而 3 期部分预计将招募约 660 名患者。该试验旨在确认 治疗 的效果大小并降低住院率,据报道,安慰剂组的住院率为 5%,而接受泽利卡帕韦治疗的患者住院率为 0%。该研究的重点是具有重大疾病负担的高危人群。
应对、状态与不确定性
该试验正在进行中,预计将于 2027 年对第一名患者进行给药并获得主要数据。该研究是一项随机、双盲、安慰剂对照试验,迄今为止,泽利卡帕韦已在 700 多人身上证明了良好的安全性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Enanta Pharmaceuticals Announces First Patient Dosed in the RESOLVE Phase 2b/3 Study of Zelicapavir, a Potential First-in-Disease Treatment for Respiratory Syncytial Virus (RSV)

News Brief
Enanta Pharmaceuticals has initiated the RESOLVE Phase 2b/3 study of zelicapavir, a potential treatment for respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) infection, by dosing the first patient on October 26, 2026. The global trial targets adults at high risk of severe RSV outcomes, including those aged 75 or older, with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) or congestive heart failure. The study aims to evaluate the efficacy and safety of zelicapavir in reducing symptom duration and hospitalization rates. Topline data from the Phase 2b portion are expected in 2027, with the Phase 3 portion planned to enroll approximately 660 patients. Zelicapavir, which inhibits the RSV N-protein, has shown a favorable safety profile in over 700 participants and may offer a first-in-disease antiviral treatment for RSV. The trial is designed to confirm the treatment effect size and generate additional clinical evidence, with the potential to significantly impact RSV management in high-risk populations.
Original source text (English)
Global Phase 2b/3 Study Will Evaluate Symptom Duration and Hospitalization Rates in Adult Outpatients at High Risk of Severe Outcomes from RSV Topline Phase 2b Data Expected in 2027 WATERTOWN, Mass. --(BUSINESS WIRE)--Oct. 26, 2026-- Enanta Pharmaceuticals , Inc. (NASDAQ:ENTA), a clinical-stage biotechnology company dedicated to creating small molecule drugs for viral infections and immunological diseases, today announced that the first patient has been dosed in RESOLVE, its global Phase 2b/3 clinical trial of zelicapavir in adults at high risk of severe outcomes from RSV infection. Topline data from the Phase 2b portion of the study are expected in 2027. “We are excited to advance our zelicapavir program into a Phase 2b/3 study with the dosing of the first patient in RESOLVE,” said Scott T. Rottinghaus , M.D., Chief Medical Officer of Enanta Pharmaceuticals . “Based on learnings from our Phase 2 RSVHR study, the RESOLVE trial is enrolling adults at high risk for severe outcomes from RSV. These patients with congestive heart failure, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, or age of 75 or older have substantial disease burden and the potential to benefit from an RSV treatment. The Phase 2b portion is designed to confirm the treatment effect size and generate an additional dataset with the goal of strengthening the clinical evidence supporting zelicapavir. Together with LOTUS, our pediatric Phase 2b trial, this progress positions us to deliver two important Phase 2b data readouts in 2027 and marks a significant step forward in the development of zelicapavir. With no approved RSV antiviral therapies currently available, these advancements underscore the potential for zelicapavir to become a first-in-disease antiviral treatment.” "RSV remains a significant cause of illness, particularly among older adults and individuals with underlying health conditions who are at heightened risk of severe disease. Despite RSV’s burden, there are no treatment options available,” said Juan Antonio Carmona Herrera , M.D., a Principal Investigator in the RESOLVE study and President of C&A Clinical Trials Corp. “An effective antiviral has the potential to change the way RSV is managed by directly targeting the virus, with the goal of reducing disease severity, preventing complications, and improving patient outcomes. The development of a treatment specifically for RSV would represent an important advancement for physicians and the millions of patients at risk of serious illness each year." The Phase 2b/3 randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter global trial will evaluate the efficacy and safety of zelicapavir in adult outpatients who test positive for RSV and have had respiratory tract infection symptoms for no more than 3 days. Patients will have at least one of the following risk factors: 75 years of age or older, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, or congestive heart failure. These risk factors are the same as the HR3 population in the Company’s previous Phase 2 RSVHR trial where a one-week reduction in time to complete resolution of symptoms, as well as a reduction in hospitalization, were observed. Patients will receive an oral dose of 800mg of zelicapavir or placebo once daily for 7 days and be evaluated for 28 days thereafter. The primary endpoint is the time to complete resolution of all 13 RSV symptoms as measured by the RiiQTM tool. A key secondary endpoint will evaluate hospitalization rate, with other secondary endpoints of additional clinical efficacy measures, antiviral activity, and safety of zelicapavir. The Phase 2b portion of the study will include a minimum of 200 patients and is designed to confirm the treatment effect size on the primary and key secondary endpoints in the targeted population, while the Phase 3 portion of the study is expected to enroll approximately 660 patients. About Zelicapavir Zelicapavir is being developed for the treatment of RSV infection and has been granted Fast Track designation by the U.S. Food and Drug Administration . Zelicapavir inhibits the RSV N-protein with nanomolar activity against both RSV-A and RSV-B. This mechanism is differentiated from RSV fusion inhibitors as targeting the N-protein inhibits viral replication versus viral entry and has a high barrier to resistance. In a Phase 2 trial of hospitalized and non-hospitalized pediatric RSV patients aged 28 days to 3 years old, zelicapavir demonstrated an antiviral effect and shortened time to complete resolution of RSV symptoms. In a Phase 2 trial of high-risk adults, a clinically meaningful improvement in time to complete resolution of all 13 RSV symptoms was observed for zelicapavir compared to placebo, with a benefit of 6.7 days for patients with congestive heart failure, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, or age 75 years or older, termed the HR3 population. Importantly, zelicapavir treatment resulted in a lower hospitalization rate, with an RSV-related hospitalization rate of 5% in placebo, compared to 0% in patients who received zelicapavir. Throughout its clinical development program, zelicapavir has demonstrated a favorable safety profile and has been well-tolerated in more than 700 people to date. About RSV RSV is the most common cause of bronchiolitis (inflammation of the small airways in the lung) and pneumonia in children under one year of age in the United States and a significant cause of respiratory illness in older adults and immunocompromised individuals. 1,2 According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention , virtually all children in the United States get an RSV infection by the time they are two years old. 3 RSV represents a significant health threat for adults older than 50 years of age, with up to 180,000 hospitalizations associated with RSV infections annually in the United States . 2 Overall, in the United States , RSV accounts for up to 6.8 million outpatient visits and approximately 370,000 hospitalizations, with 24,000 deaths. 4 About Enanta Pharmaceuticals, Inc. Enanta is using its robust, chemistry-driven approach and drug discovery capabilities to become a leader in the discovery and development of small molecule drugs for viral infections and immunological diseases. In virology, Enanta’s clinical programs are focused on the development of first-in-disease and best-in-disease treatments for respiratory syncytial virus (RSV). The Company’s immunology pipeline aims to develop treatments for inflammatory diseases by targeting key drivers of the type 2 immune response, with KIT, STAT6 and MRGPRX2 inhibition. Glecaprevir, a protease inhibitor discovered by Enanta, is part of the only treatment approved for curing both acute and chronic hepatitis C virus (HCV) infection and is sold by AbbVie in numerous countries under the tradenames MAVYRET® (U.S.) and MAVIRET® (ex-U.S.) (glecaprevir/pibrentasvir). A portion of Enanta’s royalties from HCV products developed under its collaboration with AbbVie contribute ongoing funding to Enanta’s operations. Please visit www.enanta.com for more information. Time
The first patient was dosed in the RESOLVE Phase 2b/3 study on October 26, 2026. Topline Phase 2b data are expected in 2027.
Location and population
Global Phase 2b/3 clinical trial in adult outpatients at high risk of severe outcomes from RSV infection, including those aged 75 or older, with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, or congestive heart failure.
Event
Enanta Pharmaceuticals announced the dosing of the first patient in the RESOLVE Phase 2b/3 study of zelicapavir, a potential treatment for RSV infection. The study is designed to evaluate symptom duration and hospitalization rates in high-risk adults.
Scale, impact and risk
The Phase 2b portion of the study will include a minimum of 200 patients, while the Phase 3 portion is expected to enroll approximately 660 patients. The trial aims to confirm the treatment effect size and reduce hospitalization rates, with a reported 5% hospitalization rate in the placebo group compared to 0% in zelicapavir-treated patients. The study is focused on high-risk populations with significant disease burden.
Response, status and uncertainty
The trial is ongoing, with the first patient dosed and topline data expected in 2027. The study is a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial, and zelicapavir has demonstrated a favorable safety profile in over 700 people to date.

加强监测并在越南测试呼吸道合胞病毒。- 越南.vn

Strengthening surveillance and testing for respiratory syncytial virus in Vietnam. - Vietnam.vn
新闻简报The article reports on a scientific workshop held on October 22nd, 2026, in Hanoi, Vietnam, organized by the National Institute of Hygiene and Epidemiology. The workshop aimed to strengthen surveillance and testing for respiratory syncytial virus (RSV), a common cause of respiratory infections, particularly in children under two and the elderly with underlying health conditions. RSV is a significant factor in respiratory tract infections among hospitalized children, with detection rates of 26% at Children's Hospital 1 and 24% at the Tropical Diseases Hospital. Globally, in 2019, RSV caused 33 million cases of lower respiratory tract infections in children under five, including 3.6 million hospitalizations and 101,400 deaths. The article highlights the need for improved surveillance, testing, and preventive measures, especially for vulnerable groups. Dr. Ngo Duy Nghia and Dr. Vu Ngoc Long emphasized the importance of monitoring RSV circulation, identifying risk groups, and enhancing coordination between health systems to improve data quality and early warning systems. The response to RSV is currently under investigation and requires further implementation to address the disease's high burden and mortality risks.
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研讨会于2026年10月22日上午在越南河内举行。活动日期为2026年10月22日。发布日期为2026年10月22日。
地点与对象
该活动在越南河内举行,涉及国家卫生研究所和 流行病学。受影响人群包括婴儿、5岁以下儿童以及有基础健康问题的老年人。
事件
国家卫生研究所和 流行病学 组织了一次科学研讨会,讨论 监测 和 呼吸道合胞病毒 (RSV) 在越南的测试。活动根据研讨会的标题和内容确定。
规模、影响与风险
RSV is a significant cause of respiratory tract infections in hospitalized children in Vietnam, with a detection rate of 26% at Children's Hospital 1 and 24% at the Tropical Diseases Hospital. In children under 2 years old with acute bronchiolitis, 50.65% of cases were RSV-related. Globally, in 2019, there were 33 million cases of RSV-related lower respiratory tract infections, including 3.6 million hospitalizations and approximately 101,400 deaths. The risk is high, especially for infants under six months old and the elderly with underlying health conditions.
应对、状态与不确定性
研讨会的重点是提高监测、测试能力和实施预防措施。Ngo Duy Nghia 和 Vu Ngoc Long 博士强调需要监测 RSV 传播、识别风险群体以及加强预防性卫生系统和医疗机构之间的协调。该回应正在调查中,需要进一步落实。

Original source text (English)
English Login Homepage Topic News Political System Destination Event Travel Happy Vietnam Enterprise Product Heritage Museum Figure Multimedia Data Home Culture – Society Y tế Strengthening surveillance and testing for respiratory syncytial virus in Vietnam. On the morning of October 22nd, in Hanoi, the National Institute of Hygiene and Epidemiology organized a scientific workshop titled "Surveillance and Testing of Respiratory Syncytial Virus (RSV) in Vietnam: Current Situation, Gaps, and Directions for Enhancement". Báo Quân đội Nhân dân•22/10/2026 Follow Vietnam.vn on Google Add Vietnam.vn on Google 0 The workshop focused on assessing the burden of disease caused by RSV, and also discussed surveillance, testing, and solutions to improve the effectiveness of disease prevention and control in Vietnam. Dr. Ngo Duy Nghia, Head of the Department of Infectious Disease Control, National Institute of Hygiene and Epidemiology, presented his paper at the conference. Speaking at the workshop, Dr. Ngo Duy Nghia, Head of the Department of Infectious Disease Control at the National Institute of Hygiene and Epidemiology, stated that respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) is one of the most common causes of respiratory infections, primarily transmitted through droplets and contact with contaminated surfaces. Most children have been infected with RSV before the age of two, with infants, especially those under six months old, having a higher risk of severe illness and death. The elderly and those with underlying health conditions are also vulnerable to infection with this virus. Alarmingly, the burden of RSV-related disease worldwide remains high. According to research presented at the conference, in 2019, an estimated 33 million cases of RSV-related lower respiratory tract infections occurred globally in children under 5 years old, including 3.6 million hospitalizations and approximately 101,400 deaths. Notably, 46% of RSV-related deaths occurred in children under 6 months old, highlighting the severity of the disease for young children. Dr. Vu Ngoc Long, representing the Department of Disease Prevention ( Ministry of Health ), presented a paper at the conference. In Vietnam, research results also show that RSV is a significant factor in cases of respiratory tract infections in hospitalized children. According to studies cited by the reporter, the detection rate of RSV in hospitalized children at Children's Hospital 1 was 26%, and at the Tropical Diseases Hospital it was 24%. A study on children under 2 years old with acute bronchiolitis also recorded RSV infection in 50.65% of cases. You may also like Prenatal care helps protect both mother and baby from infectious diseases.According to experts, the first six months of life are a period that creates an "immunity gap" because the baby's immune system is not yet fully developed and their airways are still small, making them susceptible to respiratory illnesses. Immunization gap and disease burden due to RSV virusThe increase in respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) infections in children, especially infants, has very serious and unpredictable consequences with a high mortality rate. Treatment is difficult because there is currently no specific drug. A kiss from an adult could send a newborn to the intensive care unit.Many parents are shocked when their healthy children suddenly develop severe respiratory distress after only a few days of common coughing and runny noses. This is due to the "immunity gap" in the first six months of life, which allows infants to become infected with respiratory syncytial virus (RSV). Besides causing a significant burden of disease, RSV also exhibits different distribution patterns across regions. In the North, the virus typically circulates strongly from October to March of the following year; in the Central region from July to November, while in the South it is mainly from May to October. This difference necessitates close monitoring of epidemiological developments in each region, improved testing capacity to detect and assess the virus's circulation trends, and proactive implementation of preventive measures. Given the risks posed by RSV, disease prevention, especially for vulnerable groups, is receiving increasing attention. Dr. Ngo Duy Nghia stated that there is currently no specific treatment for RSV. Besides personal hygiene, respiratory hygiene, and limiting contact with infected individuals, advances in medicine have opened up additional proactive prevention solutions. Overview of the scientific conference. According to the World Health Organization (WHO), RSV vaccination for pregnant women can help protect infants in the first few months of life through antibodies transferred from mother to child. In addition, long-acting monoclonal antibodies are a preventive measure against severe RSV infection in newborns. For older adults, RSV vaccination is recommended for age groups and individuals at risk. Along with preventive measures, improving infectious disease surveillance is identified as a crucial task to proactively respond to RSV. In his presentation, "Regulations on Infectious Disease Surveillance and Orientations for RSV Surveillance," Dr. Vu Ngoc Long, representing the Department of Disease Prevention (Ministry of Health), clarified current regulations on infectious disease surveillance, focusing on detection, reporting, analysis, and risk assessment of outbreaks. Regarding RSV, Dr. Vu Ngoc Long emphasized the necessity of monitoring the endemic period, identifying risk groups, and monitoring hospitalizations, severe illness, and deaths. He also stressed the need to strengthen coordination between the preventive health system and healthcare facilities to improve data quality, support early warning systems, and proactively prevent and control outbreaks. You may also like Based on the issues discussed at the workshop, strengthening epidemiological surveillance, improving testing capacity, and promoting disease prevention communication are crucial. This forms the basis for the health sector to proactively develop appropriate prevention and control measures, contributing to limiting severe cases, reducing mortality risks, and alleviating pressure on the healthcare system. 如何保护婴儿免受呼吸道合胞病毒感染

How to Protect Babies from RSV
新闻简报这篇发表于 2026 年 10 月 26 日的文章讨论了如何保护婴儿免受 呼吸道合胞病毒 (RSV) 感染,强调了其 患病率 和潜在的严重性。RSV 很常见,几乎所有 2 岁以下的儿童都会受到感染,在美国,每年有多达 80,000 名 5 岁以下儿童需要住院治疗。以预防性抗体和 疫苗接种 的出现为标志的 2025-2026 年 RSV 季节,婴儿的住院率显着下降,与大流行前的水平相比,0-2 个月大的住院率下降了 45% 至 52%,0-7 个月大的住院率下降了 28% 至 43%。该文章建议采取预防措施,例如在 RSV 季节进行免疫接种和日常卫生习惯,以降低患严重疾病的风险。它强调了认真对待 RSV 的重要性,特别是对于婴儿和幼儿,因为它是该年龄段住院的主要原因。该信息基于疾病控制和预防中心的分析,并作为护理人员和家长的指导。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该文章发表于 2026 年 10 月 26 日,在美国,RSV 季节通常从秋季开始,在冬季达到高峰。
地点与对象
美国,重点关注 5 岁以下婴儿,特别是 8 个月以下婴儿和整个 RSV 季节出生的婴儿。
事件
该文章讨论了如何保护婴儿免受 RSV 感染,强调 RSV 很常见,并且对某些婴儿来说可能很严重,并提出了预防措施和免疫选择的建议。
规模、影响与风险
RSV 每年在美国收治多达 80,000 名 5 岁以下儿童住院。与大流行前相比,2025-2026 年 RSV 季节,0-2 个月婴儿的住院率下降了 45% 至 52%,0-7 个月婴儿的住院率下降了 28% 至 43%。

建议采取免疫选择和日常卫生措施等预防措施。文章指出,这些方法是有效的,RSV 季节和预防措施的广泛使用导致住院率降低。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

How to Protect Babies from RSV

News Brief
The article, published on October 26, 2026, discusses how to protect babies from respiratory syncytial virus (RSV), emphasizing its prevalence and potential severity. RSV is common, with nearly all children infected by age 2, and can lead to hospitalization for up to 80,000 children under 5 in the United States annually. The 2025-2026 RSV season, marked by the availability of preventive antibodies and vaccinations, resulted in a significant reduction in hospitalization rates for infants, with 45% to 52% lower rates for those 0-2 months old and 28% to 43% lower rates for those 0-7 months old compared to pre-pandemic levels. The article recommends preventive measures such as immunization during RSV season and everyday hygiene practices to reduce the risk of severe illness. It stresses the importance of taking RSV seriously, especially for infants and young children, as it is the leading cause of hospitalization in this age group. The information is based on a Centers for Disease Control and Prevention analysis and is presented as guidance for caregivers and parents.
Original source text (English)

Sign Up Today My Account Dashboard Profile Saved items Logout Home About Us Contact Us Board of Directors Donate Our Donors Advertise Corrections Subscription Services Submission Forms Newsletters Privacy Policy Terms of Use News Loudoun Leesburg Education Public Safety Towns Politics Business Giving Back Obituaries Get Out Loudoun Calendar Calendar Happenings Artist Profiles Venues Taste Loudoun Moment Add Your Event Calendar Opinion Editorials Columnists Letters to the Editor Reader's Polls Sports Public Notices Donate Loudoun Now Voice Get The App Newsletter Signup Share This Facebook Twitter WhatsApp SMS Email Advertorial How to Protect Babies from RSV Oct 27, 2026 10 hrs ago 0 Facebook Twitter WhatsApp SMS Email Facebook Twitter WhatsApp SMS Email Print Copy article link Save Facebook Twitter WhatsApp SMS Email Print Copy article link Save (StatePoint) Respiratory syncytial virus, or RSV, infects people of all ages, however, it can be dangerous for babies and some young children. As many as 80,000 children under 5 in the United States are hospitalized each year due to the disease. Fortunately, there are proven-effective methods to significantly reduce severe illness for babies. Here’s what the American Lung Association, with support from Sanofi, wants caregivers, parents, and expectant parents to know about protecting their babies from RSV: RSV is common. RSV is so common that by age 2, nearly 100% of children will be infected. Most people, including infants, develop only mild symptoms, like that of a common cold. For some though, RSV can be severe and even life threatening. All infants are at increased risk for severe illness. If a child does come down with RSV and they are having difficulty breathing, not drinking enough, or their symptoms are getting worse, it’s time to seek emergency care. RSV season is now. The RSV season typically starts in the fall and peaks in the winter in the United States. Because of this, parents should take action to protect their infants, especially those under eight months old and those born throughout the entire season. There are multiple ways to protect infants. It is recommended that all babies be protected from severe RSV by one of these two immunization options during RSV season (most babies do not need both): • A preventive antibody for infants younger than 8 months old and born during or entering their first RSV season. Preventive antibodies are not vaccines, but rather proteins that mimic the immune system’s naturally produced antibodies to help fight off harmful viruses. A single dose provides protection for at least five months and begins working right away. Young children between 8 and 19 months at increased risk for severe RSV symptoms, including children with chronic lung disease, are recommended to receive an RSV preventive antibody. • An RSV vaccination received during weeks 32 through 36 weeks of pregnancy. Protection lasts about six months after birth. These methods are effective. Marking the second RSV season with widespread availability of both preventive options, 2025-2026 resulted in a reduced risk of severe RSV and RSV-related hospitalizations in infants. A Centers for Disease Control and Prevention analysis of RSV hospitalization rates among two surveillance groups from October 2024 through February 2025 found hospitalization rates 45% and 52% lower for infants 0-2 months during peak RSV season (December through February) and 28% and 43% lower for those 0-7 months old, compared to pre-pandemic (2018) rates. Preventive actions can also help. RSV is spread person-to-person via coughing and sneezing, by touching objects that have the virus on them, and by direct contact, like when kissing a baby while sick with RSV. That is why parents and caregivers of babies are also encouraged to follow everyday preventive actions like: • Avoiding close contact with sick people • Covering coughs and sneezes with a tissue • Washing hands often with soap and water for 20 seconds • Cleaning frequently touched surfaces • Staying home and avoiding close contact with others when sick • Avoiding shared cups, bottles or toys For more information and to learn more, visit Lung.org/rsv. Severe RSV can be unpredictable. As the leading cause of hospitalization in infants, it is vital for parents and caregivers to take RSV seriously, and to protect their babies. ***** Facebook Twitter WhatsApp SMS Email Print Copy article link Save × Post a comment as anonymous Emoticons [smile] [beam] [wink] [sad] [cool] [innocent] [rolleyes] [whistling] [lol] [huh] [tongue] [love] [sleeping] [yawn] [unsure] [angry] [blink] [crying] [ohmy] [scared] [sleep] [sneaky] [tongue_smile] [thumbdown] [thumbup] [censored] [happybirthday] [ban] [spam] [offtopic] [batman] [ninja] [pirate] [alien] Comment Text × Report × Watch this discussion. CDC:近1/10的呼吸道合胞病毒成人患者在医院或出院后30天内死亡

CDC: Almost 1 in 10 adults with RSV died at hospital or within 30 days of discharge
新闻简报CDC 报告称,根据 IDWeek 年会上提出的对 8 个 RSV 季节的分析,8.6% 因 呼吸道合胞病毒 (RSV) 住院的成年人在住院期间或出院 30 天内死亡。该研究纳入了 2014 年至 2024 年间因 RSV 住院的 18,301 名成年人的数据,发现 死亡率 发病率在 2015-2016 年季节达到峰值 9.7%,并在 2018-2019 年下降至 7.4%。在 1,565 例死亡中,812 例发生在医院内,753 例发生在出院后 30 天内。CDC 强调,出院后的死亡风险很大,特别是对于那些未出院到临终关怀中心的患者,并呼吁对符合条件的成年人优先考虑 RSV 疫苗接种。这些发现被认为是初步的,支持更广泛的预防工作。
查看新闻五要素
时间
CDC 在 IDWeek 年会上报告了 8 个 RSV 季节的分析,死亡百分比在 2015-2016 年达到峰值 9.7%,在 2018-2019 年触底为 7.4%。该研究包括 2014 年至 2024 年的数据。
地点与对象
作为 CDC 呼吸道病毒住院 监测 网络 (RESP-NET) 的一部分,该分析检查了 2014 年至 2024 年间美国因 RSV 住院的 18,301 名成年人的数据。
事件
CDC 报告称,根据对 8 个 RSV 季节的分析,8.6% 因 RSV 住院的成年人在住院期间或出院后 30 天内死亡。其中包括 812 例住院期间死亡和 753 例出院后 30 天内死亡。
规模、影响与风险
在报告的 1,565 例死亡中,286 人出院到临终关怀医院,467 人(2.6%)在出院到其他地点后死亡。CDC 强调了住院后 死亡率 风险的增加,并强调了 RSV 疫苗接种 对符合条件的成年人的重要性。
应对、状态与不确定性
CDC 在 IDWeek 年会上公布了这些初步结果,支持有关 RSV 疫苗接种 的预防信息。研究结果基于 监测 数据,被认为是初步的。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

CDC: Almost 1 in 10 adults with RSV died at hospital or within 30 days of discharge

News Brief
The CDC reported that 8.6% of adults hospitalized with respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) died during hospitalization or within 30 days of discharge, based on an analysis of eight RSV seasons presented at the IDWeek annual meeting. The study, which included data from 18,301 adults hospitalized with RSV between 2014 and 2024, found that the mortality rate peaked at 9.7% in the 2015-2016 season and dropped to 7.4% in 2018-2019. Of the 1,565 deaths, 812 occurred in the hospital and 753 within 30 days post-discharge. The CDC emphasized that the risk of death in the post-hospitalization period is significant, particularly for those not discharged to hospice, and called for prioritizing RSV vaccination for eligible adults. These findings are considered preliminary and support broader prevention efforts.
Original source text (English)

CDC: Almost 1 in 10 adults with RSV died at hospital or within 30 days of discharge Foster Stubbs (Photo: South_agency/Getty Images) October 26, 2026 Share › Share on Facebook Share on Twitter Share on LinkedIn Share by Email Print (Photo: South_agency/Getty Images) A total of 8.6% of adults hospitalized with respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) died during hospitalization or within the 30 days after they were discharged, according to CDC analysis of eight RSV seasons reported at the IDWeek annual meeting. The analysis examined data from 18,301 adults hospitalized with RSV at some point between 2014 and 2024; they were included in the CDC’s Respiratory Virus Hospitalization Surveillance Network (RESP-NET). Illness attributable to RSV infection can range from mild cold-like signs and symptoms to pneumonia or bronchiolitis. RSV vaccination in adults began during the 2023-2024 season, and the CDC recommends a one-time, single dose of any of three Food and Drug Administration-licensed vaccines for all adults ages 75 or older, or those age 50-74 who are at greater risk of severe RSV. The percentage of adults hospitalized with RSV who died peaked at 9.7% in 2015-2016 and bottomed out at 7.4% in 2018-2019. Of the 1,565 deaths reported during hospitalization or within 30 days of discharge, 812 occurred in the hospital and 753 came within 30 days post-discharge. Among those who died after discharge, 286 were discharged to hospice. Meanwhile, 467 (2.6%) people died after being discharged to somewhere other than hospice. “We think [people being discharged to somewhere other than hospice] is the most concerning, as these patients were likely discharged home or to some higher level of care — but they were probably not expected to die within a month of the hospitalization with RSV infection,” Michael Melgar, MD, of the CDC in Atlanta told MedPage Today. “These preliminary results speak to an increased mortality risk in the immediate post-hospitalization period and support overarching prevention messaging about prioritizing recommended RSV vaccination for eligible adults.” Top Stories View All News Senators allege abuse, deficiencies will be missed under Risk-Based Surveys News Also in the News for Wednesday, Oct. 28 News Why CMS nursing home chief says more past noncompliance citations may be coming Editors' Picks View All Understaffed but ineligible: Is CMS missing a chance to lift up safety-net nursing homes? RSV 与流感:如何区分

RSV vs. flu: How to tell the difference
新闻简报Stacker 于 2026 年 10 月 23 日发表的这篇文章提供了 呼吸道合胞病毒 (RSV) 和流感 (flu) 之间的比较。报告解释说,这两种疾病都是传染性呼吸道感染,在寒冷的月份达到高峰,并可能导致轻微到严重的症状,特别是在婴儿、老年人和免疫系统较弱的人群中。RSV 通常会导致幼儿出现 毛细支气管炎 和肺炎,而流感会引起高烧和身体疼痛等全身症状,并可能导致肺炎等并发症。该文章强调了区分这两种病毒的重要性,因为它们是由不同的病原体引起的,需要采取不同的 治疗 和预防方法。它还强调了公共卫生运动在促进 疫苗接种、手部卫生和生病时呆在家里以减少这两种疾病传播的作用。医疗保健提供者建议对 RSV 和流感进行准确的 诊断 检测和支持护理,如果尽早服用,诊断 药物对流感有效。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该文章发表于 2026 年 10 月 23 日,讨论了 RSV 和流感的感染高峰季节,通常发生在寒冷的月份。
地点与对象
这篇文章本质上是一般性的,没有具体说明特定地点或人群,但它提到了婴儿、老年人和免疫系统较弱的个人等弱势群体。
事件
本文对 RSV 和流感进行了比较分析,解释了它们的症状、传播 和 治疗 方法。它不是 暴发 或特定事件的报告,而是一般信息。
规模、影响与风险
文章指出,呼吸道合胞病毒可导致弱势群体严重呼吸窘迫,而流感可引起肺炎等并发症,并使慢性健康状况恶化。它还提到流感可以迅速变异,需要每年 疫苗接种。
应对、状态与不确定性
该文章重点介绍了 疫苗接种、手部卫生以及生病时呆在家里以防止 RSV 和流感传播等公共卫生建议。它还提到,医疗保健提供者建议测试准确的 诊断,并且 治疗 选项根据病毒和严重程度而有所不同。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

RSV vs. flu: How to tell the difference

News Brief
This article, published on October 23, 2026, by Stacker, provides a comparison between respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) and influenza (flu). It explains that both are contagious respiratory infections that peak during colder months and can cause mild to severe symptoms, particularly in vulnerable populations such as infants, older adults, and those with weakened immune systems. While RSV often leads to bronchiolitis and pneumonia in young children, the flu can cause systemic symptoms like high fever and body aches, and may lead to complications such as pneumonia. The article emphasizes the importance of distinguishing between the two viruses, as they are caused by different pathogens and require distinct approaches to treatment and prevention. It also highlights the role of public health campaigns in promoting vaccination, hand hygiene, and staying home when sick to reduce the spread of both diseases. Healthcare providers recommend testing for accurate diagnosis and supportive care for both RSV and flu, with antiviral medications being effective for the flu if taken early.
Original source text (English)
Home News Data Centers Local News Regional News National News Crime Education Business Special Reports Ag News Voter Guide 2026 Politics Voter Resources Full Election Results Fuel Finder Weather KQ2 Storm Tracker Weather Alert Days Current Road Conditions Interactive Radar Closings and Delays Weather Call Weather Wise Sports KQ2 Game of the Week High School Sports College Sports Missouri Tigers Kansas City Chiefs Watch Newscast Livestream Livestream Special Coverage Community Nurse of the Month Locals 2 Know Weekend 2 Do’s Your Events Project Safe Family Don’t Drive Distracted Adopt A Pet Deals Contests Pay It Forward Fiesta Before Noon Best of St. Joe Jobs Work for KQ2/NPG Explore Local Jobs Obituaries Share About Us Download Our Apps Advertise with Us Meet the Newsroom TV Listings Submit News Tip Newsletter Sign-up Closed Captioning EEO Public File FCC Public File Skip to Content News Weather Sports Deals Contests Obituaries Watch Share St. Joseph 69° Stacker-Medical Care RSV vs. flu: How to tell the difference By Stacker Published October 23, 2026 3:47 pm RSV and the flu are both contagious respiratory infections that tend to circulate heavily during the fall and winter months. They can cause symptoms ranging from mild to severe, especially in young children, older adults, and those with weakened immune systems. Despite some overlap in symptoms, these viruses are caused by different pathogens and require distinct approaches to treatment and prevention. Awareness of their differences is crucial for effective management and care, especially during peak infection seasons. Below, Doctronic explains how to tell the difference between RSV and the flu. Respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) and influenza (flu) share similar symptoms but affect people differently. Understanding the key differences helps in seeking timely and appropriate care. Early recognition of symptoms can prevent complications, especially in vulnerable populations. Knowing when to consult a healthcare provider is crucial for recovery and safety. What Is RSV? Respiratory syncytial virus, or RSV, is a common virus that infects the respiratory tract. It is especially known for causing bronchiolitis and pneumonia in infants and young children. Most healthy adults experience mild cold-like symptoms, but RSV can lead to serious illness in vulnerable groups. The virus is highly contagious and spreads through respiratory droplets when an infected person coughs or sneezes. It can survive on surfaces for several hours, making it easy for the virus to spread in crowded environments like daycare centers and schools. RSV infections typically peak during the colder months, and while many children will contract RSV by their second birthday, most will recover without requiring hospitalization. For some, particularly those with pre-existing health conditions, RSV can lead to severe respiratory distress. Healthcare providers often recommend supportive care, such as hydration and fever management, to help alleviate symptoms. In recent years, research has focused on developing vaccines and treatments to reduce the impact of RSV, especially for high-risk populations. What Is the Flu? Influenza, commonly called the flu, is caused by influenza viruses that infect the nose, throat, and sometimes the lungs. It tends to cause more systemic symptoms like high fever, body aches, and fatigue. The flu can also lead to complications such as pneumonia and worsening of chronic health conditions. Unlike RSV, the flu can mutate rapidly, leading to different strains each season, which is why annual vaccinations are recommended to provide the best protection against the most prevalent strains. The flu virus spreads in similar ways to RSV, through respiratory droplets and contaminated surfaces. Symptoms usually appear suddenly and can be severe, often leading individuals to seek medical attention. Antiviral medications can be effective in reducing the duration and severity of flu symptoms if taken early in the course of the illness. Public health campaigns emphasize the importance of vaccination, hand hygiene, and staying home when sick to prevent the spread of the flu, particularly among vulnerable populations such as the elderly and those with chronic health issues. Key Symptom Differences Between RSV and Flu While RSV and the flu share some symptoms, there are subtle differences that can help you tell them apart. Common Symptoms of RSV Runny nose and nasal congestion Coughing and wheezing Fever, usually mild to moderate Rapid or troubled breathing, especially in infants Decreased appetite and irritability in young children Common Symptoms of the Flu Sudden onset of high fever Severe muscle and body aches Chills and sweats Fatigue and weakness lasting several days Dry cough and sore throat How Symptoms Progress RSV symptoms often start with a runny nose and mild cough, then can progress to wheezing and breathing difficulties. The flu usually hits fast with a high fever and intense body aches, followed by respiratory symptoms. Recognizing these patterns can guide you in seeking the right care. Who Is Most at Risk? Both RSV and flu can affect anyone, but certain groups face higher risks of severe illness. High-Risk Groups for RSV Infants, especially those under 6 months Premature babies Older adults, particularly those over 65 years old People with chronic lung or heart conditions Individuals with weakened immune systems High-Risk Groups for Flu Young children under 5 years, especially under 2 years Adults over 65 years Pregnant women People with chronic illnesses like asthma, diabetes, or heart disease Residents of nursing homes or long-term care facilities Diagnosis and When to Seek Care Because symptoms overlap, professional diagnosis is important. Testing can confirm whether RSV or flu is causing the illness, guiding treatment decisions. How Diagnosis Is Made Doctors often use nasal or throat swabs to test for RSV and flu viruses. These tests can deliver results quickly, sometimes within minutes. If you or your child is experiencing severe symptoms or worsening breathing, prompt medical evaluation is essential. Treatment Options for RSV and Flu Treatment varies depending on the virus and the severity of symptoms. Managing RSV There is no specific antiviral treatment for RSV in most cases. Care focuses on relieving symptoms and supporting breathing. This may include: Using a humidifier to ease breathing Ensuring adequate fluid intake Monitoring oxygen levels in severe cases Hospitalization for infants or those with respiratory distress Managing the Flu Antiviral medications can reduce the severity and duration of flu if started early. Supportive care includes rest, hydration, and fever management. Vaccination remains the most effective way to prevent the flu each season. When to Contact a Doctor Difficulty breathing or rapid breathing High fever that does not respond to medication Persistent chest pain or wheezing Dehydration signs like dry mouth or little urine output Symptoms worsening after initial improvement Preventing RSV and Flu Prevention strategies overlap but have some differences. Preventing RSV Frequent hand washing Avoiding close contact with sick individuals Cleaning and disinfecting surfaces Limiting the exposure of infants to crowded places during the RSV season Vaccine for eligible individuals Preventing the Flu Annual flu vaccination for everyone over 6 months Good respiratory hygiene, like covering coughs and sneezes Staying home when sick to avoid spreading the virus Take the Next Step Toward Respiratory Care Knowing the differences between RSV and the flu can help you respond quickly and appropriately. Both illnesses can be serious, but early recognition and care improve outcomes. Frequently Asked Questions Can RSV and flu occur at the same time? Yes, it is possible to be infected with both RSV and flu simultaneously, which can increase the severity of symptoms. Medical evaluation is important if symptoms worsen suddenly. Is there a vaccine for RSV? Yes, there is a vaccine for certain eligible individuals. How long do RSV and flu symptoms last? RSV symptoms typically last one to two weeks, while flu symptoms often resolve within a week, but fatigue can linger longer. When should children with RSV or flu see a doctor? Seek medical care if your child has difficulty breathing, a high fever not responding to medication, dehydration, or worsening symptoms. Can adults get severe RSV infections? Yes, older adults and those with chronic health conditions can experience severe RSV infections requiring medical attention. This story was produced by Doctronic and reviewed and distributed by Stacker. "Coughing all night, our baby couldn't sleep"... Rising number of 'infant and toddler RSV' patients during seasonal transition

News Brief
In South Korea, the number of RSV (Respiratory Syncytial Virus) hospitalizations among infants and young children has surged, particularly in Week 42 with 60 cases. Symptoms are similar to the common cold but can worsen in infants under 12 months, potentially leading to pneumonia and respiratory distress. Health experts recommend maintaining indoor humidity and considering preventive antibody injections to reduce the risk of severe illness and hospitalization.

Stock Finance Industry Living Economy Tech Future theBio Entertainment RSV cases surge among infants as seasonal changes bring coughing and breathing risks Home > theBio RSV cases surge among infants as seasonal changes bring coughing and breathing risks By Hong Hyojin 2:15 p.m. Oct 23, 2026 Week 42: 60 RSV infection hospitalizations among ages 0–6 Symptoms similar to the common cold... potential for worsening into pneumonia In severe cases, symptoms such as cyanosis, dehydration, and respiratory distress may occur Number of hospitalized patients with RSV (Respiratory Syncytial Virus) based on Weeks 39–42 / Graphic by Lee Ji-hye Recently, the number of hospitalized patients with RSV (Respiratory Syncytial Virus) infection has been showing an increasing trend, particularly among infants and young children. While RSV symptoms are similar to those of the common cold, it can cause severe infections such as pneumonia in infants under 6 to 12 months of age, making prevention a critical aspect of this respiratory infectious disease. According to KDCA sentinel surveillance statistics released on the 23rd, the number of domestic RSV hospitalizations stood at 65 as of Week 42 (the 11th–17), with patients aged 0 to 6 (60 cases) accounting for the largest share. The number of hospitalizations due to RSV infection has been steadily increasing since Week 39 (the 20th–26). RSV is a common respiratory virus in infants and young children, typically circulating from autumn through the following spring. In South Korea, the epidemic usually begins in October, with patient numbers rising until March of the following year. Although symptoms resemble those of a general cold, infants under 12 months of age with weak immune systems may experience worsening conditions, such as bronchiolitis or pneumonia, which are lower respiratory tract infections. In severe cases, respiratory distress may necessitate hospitalization for treatment. In babies infected with RSV, distinct rattling sounds from phlegm and wheezing breathing typically become apparent within 3 to 4 days after the onset of coughing. Symptoms can worsen, leading to frequent nighttime coughing that prevents proper sleep or causing vomiting. Adults, including pregnant women, may experience no symptoms or only mild cold-like symptoms even when infected with RSV; however, caution is required as they may unknowingly transmit the infection to vulnerable infants. Hong Ju-hee, a pediatric specialist at H+ Yangji Hospital, emphasized, “Infants have difficulty expelling phlegm, so their conditions can deteriorate rapidly.” She added, “Caregivers should monitor for signs such as retractions (inward pulling of the skin below the ribs), dehydration (no urine output for more than 6 hours), and cyanosis (bluish discoloration of the lips and extremities).” RSV can be transmitted not only through droplets released during coughing or sneezing but also via contact with multiple pathways, such as hands or toys contaminated with the virus. In particular, postpartum care facilities where mothers and newborns live together face a risk of cluster infections if even one infected individual is present. Recently, there was a case at a postpartum care facility in Jeju where 20 people, including newborns and facility staff, were infected with RSV. To reduce the risk of hospitalization, preventive antibody injections can be considered. RSV antibody injections work by directly administering pre-formed antibodies to enhance defense against the virus. Research results are emerging indicating that lower respiratory tract infection-related hospitalizations have decreased in South Korea following the introduction of RSV preventive antibodies. Long-acting RSV preventive antibodies are known to provide approximately 5 months of protective effect with a single dose. At home, it is advisable to maintain indoor humidity at 50–60% and avoid cold air as much as possible. RSV reinfection is common, and infants with underlying conditions, such as preterm birth or chronic heart and lung diseases, are at high risk of developing severe illness. Older adults aged 65 and above may also progress to pneumonia upon RSV infection, necessitating caution regarding transmission within families. Lee Jin-cheol, Lee Deul-byeong-won (Prof.) of Seongbuk Ouria, stated, “RSV infection is highly transmissible, with most children being infected at least once before their second birthday.” He added, “Since treatment focuses on symptomatic relief of respiratory symptoms rather than specific therapy to eliminate the virus itself, preventing severe disease is paramount.” "This article was translated using AI and may differ slightly from the original." 1. Click Here Local News Manitoba's RSV vaccine rules leave some seniors seeking answers Cory Knutt, PortageOnline.com | Sunday, Oct 25 2026, 12:00 PM Share: Copied! A flu vaccine is prepared for administration as Manitobans are encouraged to get their seasonal influenza shots. Photo submitted/Rhonda Kitchen Article continues below advertisement ↴ Manitobans aged 80 and older who qualify for a free respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) vaccine cannot currently receive it at pharmacies, leaving some seniors looking for other options to access the immunization. The provincial program took effect Oct. 1, expanding access to the RSV vaccine for people aged 80 and older. Rhonda Kitchen, owner and pharmacist at Hills Drug Store, said her pharmacy has been receiving calls from patients confused about where they can get the free vaccine. Kitchen said information in the provincial letter sent to eligible seniors indicates they can receive the RSV vaccine alongside their flu and COVID-19 shots, but does not clearly explain that pharmacies cannot administer the publicly funded RSV vaccine under current regulations. “Unfortunately, it doesn't state that that cannot be at the pharmacy,” Kitchen said. RSV vaccine requires another provider Kitchen said pharmacists can administer the RSV vaccine with a doctor's prescription, but patients must pay for it in that situation. “Currently, the regulations do not allow pharmacists to provide that vaccine unless we have a prescription order from the doctor and the patients are paying for it,” she noted. That differs from the provincial program, which provides the vaccine free of charge to eligible seniors. “It does need to be done at either one of the flu clinics in their local area or at public health or through their doctor's office,” Kitchen commented. Related stories: Portage la Prairie families gather as Hill’s Drugstore marks 125 years 'Frustrated' pharmacy owner searches for fix after week-long cell phone outage Listen to this news story on the radio: Country 93 / Mix 96 / CFRY 920 Kitchen said her pharmacy has been fielding questions from patients trying to understand the difference. She recommends checking the information included with the provincial letter or contacting public health for guidance. Pharmacies continue to offer other vaccines Although pharmacies cannot currently administer the publicly funded RSV vaccine, Kitchen said they can provide several other vaccines at no cost to eligible patients. These include seasonal influenza and COVID-19 vaccines, as well as the updated pneumococcal vaccine for eligible adults aged 65 and older. Pharmacists can also provide tetanus, diphtheria and pertussis boosters when patients are due for one, generally every 10 years. Where seniors can get help Kitchen recommends that people with questions about their RSV vaccine eligibility or where to receive the publicly funded dose contact Manitoba Public Health at 204-856-2040. 随着呼吸道病毒季节的临近,顶级医生发表了意见

Top doctors weigh in as respiratory virus seasons looms
新闻简报安大略省西南部的公共卫生官员正在为即将到来的呼吸道病毒季节做准备,其中包括监测 呼吸道合胞病毒 (RSV)、流感和 COVID-19。由亚历克斯·萨默斯 (Alex Summers) 领导的米德尔塞克斯-伦敦卫生部门强调,RSV 是与呼吸系统疾病“三大”并列的关键病毒之一。虽然文章概述了与该季节相关的风险,但没有提供 RSV 的具体病例或死亡人数。准备工作包括为潜在的传播做好准备和管理卫生系统的影响。该信息基于联合摘要,无法查看完整的原始文章正文。重点仍然是 RSV 作为主要目标,在更广泛的呼吸道季节背景下提到了流感和 COVID-19 等相关实体。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Top doctors weigh in as respiratory virus seasons looms

News Brief
Public health officials in Southwestern Ontario are preparing for the upcoming respiratory virus season, which includes monitoring for respiratory syncytial virus (RSV), influenza, and COVID-19. The Middlesex-London Health Unit, led by Alex Summers, emphasizes that RSV is one of the key viruses being tracked, alongside the 'big three' of respiratory illnesses. While the article outlines the risks associated with the season, it does not provide specific case or death counts for RSV. The preparation efforts include readiness for potential spread and managing health-system impacts. The information is based on a syndicated summary, and the full original article body was not available for review. The focus remains on RSV as the primary target, with related entities like influenza and COVID-19 mentioned in the context of the broader respiratory season.

Matthew Daldalian/The London Free Press By Brian Williams October 27, 2026 at 12:28 p.m. As Southwestern Ontario and the rest of the province gets set to enter respiratory virus season, public health offices are preparing for the spread of illnesses, including COVID-19 and influenza. Here are some things to know about the risks ahead. WHICH RESPIRATORY VIRUSES ARE MOST CLOSELY MONITORED? The respiratory illnesses most closely monitored by the Middlesex-London Health Unit are influenza, COVID-19 and respiratory syncytial virus (RSV), said Alex Summers, the health unit’s medical officer of health. Summers said there are other “common cold respiratory viruses” that will circulate over respiratory illness season, but those are the “big three” the public health office monitors.
